```text
188	\paragraph{Allocation Behavior.}
189	Table~\ref{tab:phase} (Appendix~\ref{app:alloc_full}) reveals non-monotonic allocation preference: uniform is best at $\leq$30\% ($7.3\times$ smaller MMLU $\sigma$), global at 35--40\%, both collapse at $\geq$45\%. % exp_id: kl30_3seed, regime_kl35_global, regime_kl35_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform
190	
191	\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}
192	\label{sec:cross_arch}
193	On Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}, PPL generalizes ($-$17.3\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_arch}); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but MMLU/GSM8K decline (Appendix~\ref{app:14b}).
194	Consistent regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, 14B GSM8K 32.2$\pm$5.3 vs.\ 52.1) suggests systematic limitations of output-distribution KL. The pipeline adds 6.4\% latency overhead absent sparse kernels (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456, mistral_teal30_downstream, qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform, latency_profiling
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Model and Predictor.}\nWe evaluate on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, which uses SwiGLU FFN blocks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} with $d_{\\text{model}}=4096$ and $d_{\\text{ff}}=14336$ across 32 layers.\nThe sparsity predictor is a two-layer MLP with 2.37M parameters per layer (75.9M total), implemented as Linear($4096 \\to 128$) + GELU + Linear($128 \\to 14336$), trained to produce per-neuron importance scores that minimize output-distribution divergence under sparsity.\n\n\\paragraph{Training.}\nAll predictors are trained for 1000 steps on WikiText-103 with sequence length 2048 and batch size 4 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB), using AdamW~\\cite{loshchilov2019adamw} with learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, weight decay $1 \\times 10^{-2}$, and cosine decay.\nThe KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation.}\nWe report perplexity on WikiText-2~\\cite{merity2017pointer} (seq\\_len=2048) and downstream accuracy on ARC-Challenge (25-shot), WinoGrande (5-shot), MMLU (5-shot), GSM8K (8-shot), HellaSwag (10-shot), and PIQA (0-shot).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference Protocol.}\nAt inference, the predictor produces per-neuron scores that are passed to TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly.\nWe term this \\textit{predictor-TEAL}: the predictor replaces TEAL's magnitude-based scoring while retaining its allocation strategy.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare against: (1) \\textbf{Vanilla TEAL}~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which uses activation magnitudes directly\\footnote{TEAL is deterministic; single-run evaluation suffices. KL/BCE predictors require multi-seed evaluation due to random initialization.}; (2) \\textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; and (3) \\textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. Uniform allocation preserves downstream performance best; global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nAt 30\\% sparsity, output-distribution KL under uniform allocation achieves PPL 8.81$\\pm$0.02 (3-seed), a 14.5\\% reduction over TEAL at matched actual sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30, both at 28.5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nUniform allocation yields stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\\ref{app:stability}), while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\\pm$9.5\\%) from late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\\%, Appendix~\\ref{app:retention}; \\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform, kl30_3seed\n\n\\paragraph{Task-Type Split.}\nKL shows directional improvements over TEAL on commonsense reasoning (ARC-c, WG, HellaSwag, PIQA: 4/4 wins at 30\\% uniform; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive tasks (MMLU, GSM8K: 0/2).\nWith only three seeds, 95\\% confidence intervals for individual downstream tasks overlap; we therefore treat PPL ($\\sigma{=}0.02$, non-overlapping) as the primary metric and downstream differences as directional evidence.\nThe KL predictor protects L14--L18 (14--18\\% sparsity vs.\\ 32\\% global mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:perlayer_sparsity}) while aggressively pruning L22--L28 (48--57\\%). Constraint interventions (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}) confirm a task-dependent trade-off: removing the valley improves MMLU +3.4pp but reduces ARC-c by 1.5pp, consistent with localized factual associations~\\cite{geva2023dissecting}; task-aware allocation may resolve this. % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, teal30_uniform, hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform, perlayer_sparsity_analysis, constraint_protect_late, constraint_remove_valley\n\n\\paragraph{Knowledge-Task Regression: Hypothesis.}\nConstraint experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}) show allocation contributes: removing the learned valley improves MMLU +3.4pp and GSM8K +1.6pp. A C4-trained control (Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_control}) does not alleviate the regression (MMLU 40.0\\%, GSM8K 4.1\\% vs.\\ 40.7\\%, 7.5\\%), suggesting the deficit is structural to the optimization objective rather than training data composition. Both loss-inherent (mode-covering sacrifices rare activations) and allocation-induced factors likely contribute; disentangling them requires neuron-level attribution beyond our scope. % exp_id: constraint_remove_valley, c4_control_eval\n\nAt 50\\%, the PPL advantage widens (11.92 vs.\\ 23.90, 50\\% reduction) and generalizes to C4 (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}), but all methods collapse to near-random on MMLU ($\\leq$29\\%) and GSM8K ($\\leq$2.7\\%); no allocation strategy recovers at this capacity limit. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, kl50_global_s123_downstream_v2, Analysis}\n\\label{sec:mask_analysis}\n\nKL-derived masks diverge from magnitude oracles: per-layer Jaccard distance averages 68\\% at 50\\% (64 samples, 131K tokens; Figure~\\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}), with cross-seed variation (36.3\\%) confirming systematic divergence (Appendices~\\ref{app:cross_seed_jaccard},~\\ref{app:mask_viz}).\nDespite this, KL achieves PPL 11.79 vs.\\ BCE's 28.13$\\pm$0.20, confirming that distributional fidelity, not magnitude agreement, produces better masks. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bce50_3seed, c1_mask_overlap\nDivergence follows a U-shaped per-layer pattern, peaking in late layers (0.70--0.75 at 50\\%). % exp_id: c1_mask_overlap\n\n\\paragraph{Target-Swap Ablation: Target Quality vs.\\ Gradient Coupling.}\nTo disentangle whether KL's advantage stems from its coupled gradient pathway or from the mask patterns it discovers, we train a standard BCE predictor on KL-derived oracle masks instead of magnitude-based masks (C1 target-swap ablation).\nTable~\\ref{tab:c1} reports results under TEAL uniform allocation at matched sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Target-swap ablation (C1): BCE on KL-derived masks vs.\\ KL predictor (same seed), TEAL uniform. All seeds show max $\\Delta$$\\leq$1.70pp at 30\\%; max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\% (s42). $^\\dagger$\\textbf{Provenance:} s42 oracle extracted from an earlier training run (not seed-matched); s123/s456 use seed-matched retrained oracles (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle_provenance}).}\n\\label{tab:c1}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Seed & Method & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{30\\%}\n& s42$^\\dagger$ & C1 & 9.45 & 40.27 & 67.40 & \\textbf{42.30} & \\textbf{7.58} \\\\ % exp_id: c1_ablation_30pct_s42_eval_v2\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.82} & \\textbf{40.78} & \\textbf{68.59} & 40.6 & 7.35 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform\n& s123 & C1 & 8.84 & 44.71 & \\textbf{69.77} & \\textbf{39.92} & 6.82 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s123\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.83} & \\textbf{45.31} & 69.38 & 39.4 & \\textbf{7.51} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_s123_eval\n& s456 & C1 & 8.82 & 44.71 & \\textbf{69.93} & 41.04 & 6.60 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s456\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.79} & \\textbf{45.05} & 68.75 & \\textbf{42.0} & \\textbf{7.58} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_s456_uniform_eval\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%}\n& s42 & C1 & 11.96 & 32.17 & \\textbf{63.14} & 27.79 & \\textbf{2.05} \\\\ % exp_id: c1_bce_kl_targets_teal50u\n& & KL & 11.96 & \\textbf{32.59} & 62.35 & \\textbf{27.92} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: c1_kl_teal50u\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 50\\% (s42, earlier oracle), C1 matches KL within 0.79pp, while magnitude-trained BCE yields $2.4\\times$ worse PPL (Table~\\ref{tab:main}).\nAt 30\\%, retrained seeds show $\\leq$1.18pp agreement\\footnote{s42 oracle from earlier checkpoint (gap 0.65pp); s123/s456 use seed-matched converged oracles ($\\leq$0.03pp gap).}.\nC1 demonstrates that KL-discovered mask patterns \\emph{transfer} to a simpler training mechanism (PPL within 0.79pp; downstream variation up to 4.5pp MMLU); per-layer KL controls (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}) further suggest task-dependent coupling effects.\nHowever, C1 also changes mask representation: binarizing oracle soft logits ($>$0) into hard masks discards ranking information, so the near-zero delta reflects transferability of binary mask \\emph{patterns} rather than full predictor knowledge. BCE+Gumbel (32.22) underperforms standard BCE (27.97), indicating trajectory-dependent confounds that prevent clean isolation of target quality from gradient coupling (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle_provenance}). % exp_id: bce50_3seed, c1_bce_kl_targets_30u, c1_s123, bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42\n\n\\subsection{Ablation Studies}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design choice at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Ablation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$; uniform at 30\\%, global at 50\\%). $\\pm$: 3-seed; unmarked: s42. $^\\ddagger$STE: 20.8\\% actual sparsity at 30\\% target; loss effect ($\\Delta$PPL=20.4) is $5.1\\times$ relaxation effect ($\\Delta$PPL=3.98). $^\\S$MSE 50\\%: 2/3 seeds collapsed to ${\\sim}$5\\% actual sparsity; s42 only (Appendix~\\ref{app:mse_config}).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & 30\\% PPL$\\downarrow$ & 50\\% PPL$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta_{50}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{KL-based variants}} \\\\\nForward KL + Gumbel (Ours) & \\textbf{8.81$\\pm$0.02} & \\textbf{11.79} & --- \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, bench_kl50_global\nJSD (symmetric) + Gumbel & 8.90$\\pm$0.02 & 12.54 & +0.75 \\\\ % exp_id: jsd30_ablation_s42, jsd_ablation_s42\nReverse KL + Gumbel & 9.26$\\pm$0.07 & 12.80 & +1.01 \\\\ % exp_id: rkl30_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nForward KL + STE & ---$^\\ddagger$ & 15.77 & +3.98 \\\\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-layer training}} \\\\\nPer-layer MSE & 18.52$\\pm$1.68 & 229.26$^\\S$ & +217.47 \\\\ % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42\nPer-layer KL ($K{=}1$) & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 34.85 & +23.06 \\\\ % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_50_s42\nPer-layer KL ($K{=}4$) & 8.89$\\pm$0.01 & 44.23 & +32.44 \\\\ % exp_id: perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\\\\nBCE (no compensation) & 15.71 & 27.97 & +16.18 \\\\ % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42\nBCE + Gumbel (no penalty) & --- & 32.22 & +20.43 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42\nBCE + Gumbel + Penalty & --- & 38.02 & +26.23 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\nBCE + STE & --- & 60.54 & +48.75 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_ste_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Relaxation, Divergence, and SPON.}\nAt 50\\%, Gumbel-Sigmoid outperforms STE~\\cite{bengio2013ste} by 33.8\\% (11.79 vs.\\ 15.77); forward KL outperforms JSD (+6.4\\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\\%), consistent with mode-covering~\\cite{gu2024minillm}. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42, jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nAt 30\\%, forward KL (8.81) and JSD (8.90) are near-identical, both outperforming reverse KL (9.26) and MSE (18.52$\\pm$1.68). % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, jsd30_ablation_s42, rkl30_ablation_s42, mse30_uniform_s42, mse30_3seed\nKL--JSD near-equivalence suggests the divergence measure matters less than optimizing through the output distribution; coupling scope may provide additional downstream benefits (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed\n\\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Empirical Signal Type vs.\\ Coupling Scope.}\n\\label{sec:perlayer}\nTo isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.\nThe $K$-curve (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}) reveals three empirical patterns at 30\\%: (1)~PPL is non-monotonic, with $K{=}2$ worst (10.75$\\pm$0.15) due to insufficient per-layer updates and $K{=}16$ best (8.67$\\pm$0.02), marginally outperforming E2E (8.81$\\pm$0.02); (2)~at $K{=}1$ (no coupling), per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of E2E, while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse); (3)~the signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\\ MSE 18.52: 9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range ($K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\\ $K{=}2$ 10.75: 2.08 PPL).\nThis suggests output-distribution alignment contributes more than coupling scope to PPL, but the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch ($K{=}1$ masks 1/32 layers during training vs.\\ all 32 at inference), per-layer update count disparity, and the 50\\% failure where $K{=}1$ (34.85) outperforms $K{=}4$ (44.23) while E2E reaches 11.84.\nA 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): PPL and GSM8K gaps largely close while commonsense gaps (ARC-c, WG) persist, suggesting task-dependent coupling effects; detailed per-task attribution is in Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}--\\ref{app:perlayer_downstream}; full K-curve downstream in Appendix~\\ref{app:k_curve}. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global, k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{k_curve_ppl}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\caption{Coupling breadth $K$ vs.\\ perplexity (30\\%, TEAL uniform, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). $K{=}32$ denotes end-to-end training. $K{=}2$ degradation reflects insufficient per-layer updates (${\\sim}62$ vs.\\ ${\\sim}250$ at $K{=}8$).}\n\\label{fig:k_curve_ppl}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}\n\\label{sec:compensation}\nFastForward-style compensation~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) provides no benefit (Appendices~\\ref{app:comp_convergence}--\\ref{app:training_convergence}); SPON bias correction is orthogonal (Appendix~\\ref{app:spon}). Predictor capacity and temperature sensitivity in Appendix~\\ref{app:db_sensitivity}. % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42, db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep\n\n\n\\subsection{Sparsity Scaling and Regime Analysis}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_sparsity_sweep.pdf}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\caption{PPL scaling with sparsity (global allocation). KL's advantage over TEAL grows with sparsity.}\n\\label{fig:sparsity_sweep}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Allocation Behavior.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase} (Appendix~\\ref{app:alloc_full}) reveals non-monotonic allocation preference: uniform is best at $\\leq$30\\% ($7.3\\times$ smaller MMLU $\\sigma$), global at 35--40\\%, both collapse at $\\geq$45\\%. % exp_id: kl30_3seed, regime_kl35_global, regime_kl35_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}\n\\label{sec:cross_arch}\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, PPL generalizes ($-$17.3\\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch}); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but MMLU/GSM8K decline (Appendix~\\ref{app:14b}).\nConsistent regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, 14B GSM8K 32.2$\\pm$5.3 vs.\\ 52.1) suggests systematic limitations of output-distribution KL. The pipeline adds 6.4\\% latency overhead absent sparse kernels (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456, mistral_teal30_downstream, qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform, latency_profiling\n\n",
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses.
5	The KL predictor reduces PPL by 14.5\% over TEAL at 30\%, confirmed on Mistral-7B ($-$17.3\%) and Qwen-2.5-14B.
6	Gains are task-type dependent: KL improves commonsense reasoning but degrades knowledge-intensive tasks (C4 control suggests structural to the optimization objective), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL and TEAL for knowledge tasks.
7	The signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
8	Downstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$); code released upon acceptance.
9	
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our findings are bounded by four limitations.
4	\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\% is the lowest-degradation level; at 50\%, all methods collapse on reasoning tasks, and a separate predictor must be retrained per target.
5	\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$19.9pp GSM8K), suggesting output-distribution objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.
6	\textbf{Evaluation scope.} Open-ended generation (e.g., MT-Bench) and contexts beyond 2048 tokens remain untested.
7	\textbf{Baseline coverage.} The 30\% isocompute comparison lacks a BCE 2700-step baseline; WikiText-2 evaluation shares provenance with the training corpus (WikiText-103), partially mitigated by C4 OOD results (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}). MSE uses identical hyperparameters to KL; its gradient magnitude is ${\sim}5\times$ larger, and performance may improve with a dedicated learning rate.
8	
```

```text
1	\appendix

3	\section{Inference Efficiency}
4	\label{app:latency}
5	
6	Table~\ref{tab:latency} reports inference throughput on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\,GB; batch size 1, sequence length 2048, fp16, averaged over 100 forward passes).
7	
8	\begin{table}[h]
9	\centering
10	\caption{Inference latency on LLaMA-3.1-8B (RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell, batch=1, seq=2048, fp16, 100 forward passes). Overhead is relative to dense inference.}
11	\label{tab:latency}
12	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
13	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
14	\toprule
15	Method & Sparsity & ms/fwd & tok/s & Overhead \\
16	\midrule
17	Dense & 0\% & 119.11$\pm$0.57 & 17{,}195 & --- \\
18	\midrule
19	KL predictor-only & 30\% & 126.64$\pm$0.70 & 16{,}172 & +6.3\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
20	KL predictor-only & 50\% & 126.78$\pm$0.31 & 16{,}154 & +6.4\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
21	\midrule
22	Predictor + compensation & 30\% & 129.94$\pm$0.75 & 15{,}761 & +9.1\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
23	Predictor + compensation & 50\% & 129.71$\pm$0.53 & 15{,}789 & +8.9\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}}
26	\end{table}
27	
28	The full KL-sparse pipeline adds 6.4\% overhead to dense inference (126.78 vs.\ 119.11\,ms/forward), of which the predictor's 32-layer forward pass contributes 3.17\,ms (2.7\%); the remainder arises from mask application and sparse FFN indexing.
29	Eliminating the compensation network saves an additional $\sim$2.7\% latency overhead and 67.2M parameters (the per-layer GEMV required for output rescaling); output-distribution KL thus provides both quality and efficiency advantages over BCE-based approaches.
30	The overhead is near-identical at 30\% and 50\% sparsity because the predictor runs the same computation regardless of target sparsity; only the top-$k$ threshold changes.
31	
32	Note that the current implementation uses unstructured element-wise masks, which do not translate to wall-clock speedup without sparse kernel support.
33	The pipeline adds $\sim$6.4\% overhead; net acceleration requires kernel-level sparse computation optimizations (e.g., Triton block-sparse, cuSPARSE) that are beyond the scope of this work.
34	Our contribution is prediction quality and inference simplification (removing the compensation network), not end-to-end latency reduction.
35

37	\section{Predictor Bottleneck Sensitivity}
38	\label{app:db_sensitivity}
39	
40	Table~\ref{tab:db_sensitivity} reports the effect of predictor bottleneck dimension $d_b$ on PPL at 50\% sparsity under both TEAL global allocation (the paper's primary inference protocol) and per-token evaluation.
41	
42	\begin{table}[h]
43	\centering
44	\caption{Predictor bottleneck sensitivity at 50\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$). TEAL global is the primary inference protocol; per-token shown for reference.}
45	\label{tab:db_sensitivity}

47	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
48	\toprule
49	$d_b$ & Params & TEAL global & $\Delta$ & Per-token \\
50	\midrule
51	64 & 38.2M (0.48\%) & 12.076 & +0.9\% & 10.16 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
52	128 & 75.9M (0.95\%) & 11.963 & --- & 9.218 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
53	256 & 151.5M (1.89\%) & 12.105 & +1.2\% & 8.586 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
54	512 & 302.5M (3.77\%) & 12.315 & +2.9\% & 7.93 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
55	\bottomrule

58	
59	Under TEAL global allocation, increasing $d_b$ beyond 128 provides no benefit (PPL slightly worsens), while per-token evaluation shows monotonic improvement (10.16$\to$7.93).
60	This divergence indicates that the global top-$k$ allocation mechanism, not predictor capacity, is the binding constraint: additional predictor capacity produces better per-neuron scores, but the global ranking and budget distribution cannot exploit them.
61	The $d_b{=}128$ configuration (75.9M params, $<$1\% of base model) is therefore optimal for the TEAL inference protocol.

110	\section{Geometric Penalty Schedule Dynamics}
111	\label{app:lagrangian}
112	
113	Table~\ref{tab:lagrangian} reports the convergence of the geometric penalty schedule across sparsity targets.
114	The geometric doubling pattern ($1 \to 2 \to 4 \to 8 \to \ldots$) enables $\lambda$ to reach steady state within $\sim$10 steps; the deadzone ($\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\pm$2\% of the target.
115	
116	\begin{table}[h]
117	\centering
118	\caption{Geometric penalty schedule convergence across sparsity targets (margin $\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}
119	\label{tab:lagrangian}
120	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
121	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
122	\toprule
123	Target $\rho$ & $\lambda_{\max}$ & Final $\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\
124	\midrule
125	30\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\% \\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42
126	40\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\% \\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42
127	50\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\% \\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42
128	70\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\% \\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42
129	\bottomrule
130	\end{tabular}}
131	\end{table}
132	
133	At 50\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.
134	At 30\% and 70\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\lambda$ to its respective caps.
135	The 40\% target converges to $\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\% deviation; this precision reflects a regime where the target is reachable without $\lambda$ saturation.
136	
137	\begin{figure}[h]
138	\centering
139	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}
140	\caption{Lagrangian controller dynamics for 30\% sparsity target ($\lambda_{\max}=5000$). Left axis (log scale): penalty weight $\lambda$ rises from $0.01$ to its cap once actual sparsity undershoots the target at $\sim$step~150, exhibiting bang-bang oscillations before saturating. Right axis: actual sparsity (solid) converges from the natural density ($\approx$0.41) toward the target (dashed) and stabilizes near 0.28.}
141	\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	Temperature $\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.
145	A mild loss increase in the final $\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\tau \to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, consistent with convergence toward the hard-mask inference regime.
146	
147	The controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.
148	For the 30\% target, actual sparsity is $\approx$28.5\% (5\% deviation). This does not affect conclusions: TEAL at 28.5\% actual sparsity yields PPL 10.30 (vs.\ 10.97 at exact 30\%), a 0.67 PPL difference negligible relative to the KL vs.\ TEAL gap (8.96 vs.\ 10.97).

215	\section{Training Stability}
216	\label{app:stability}
217	
218	Three-seed evaluation ($s \in \{42, 123, 456\}$) at 50\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\%); training is stable across random initializations. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform
219	Downstream metrics are similarly stable: ARC-c $33.8 \pm 1.2$\%, WinoGrande $62.9 \pm 0.1$\%, MMLU $28.0 \pm 0.6$\%, GSM8K $2.2 \pm 0.1$\%.
220

222	The low standard deviation across both allocation strategies indicates that the KL training landscape is well-conditioned for this predictor architecture, with low sensitivity to initialization.
223	
224	\subsection{Cross-Seed Mask Consistency}
225	\label{app:cross_seed_jaccard}
226	
227	To assess whether KL predictors discover consistent mask patterns across initializations, we compute pairwise Jaccard distances among three independently trained KL predictors (s42/s123/s456) at 30\% uniform allocation (100 sequences, WikiText-2 validation). % exp_id: cross_seed_jaccard_kl30
228	
229	The mean cross-seed Jaccard distance is 0.363 (IoU = 0.637), substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (0.46--0.68), confirming that KL-discovered patterns form a distinct cluster from magnitude-based masks.
230	Per-layer divergence follows a U-shaped pattern: lowest at embedding-adjacent layers (L0: 0.33), peaking at early layers (L1: 0.41), with a mid-network valley (L12--14: 0.33). The random baseline Jaccard distance is 0.462.
231	
232	While specific mask configurations vary moderately across seeds (Jaccard distance 36.3\%), the resulting PPL is highly stable (8.81$\pm$0.02, CV=0.2\%), suggesting multiple near-equivalent optima in the activation mask space.
233	The cross-seed divergence (36.3\%) is substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (46--68\%), confirming that KL discovers effective patterns that cluster distinctly from magnitude-based selection.
234	
235	
236	\section{MSE Baseline Configuration}
237	\label{app:mse_config}
238	
239	The MSE predictor uses identical architecture (two-layer MLP, bottleneck $d_b{=}128$), training data (WikiText-103, 1000 steps), optimizer (AdamW, lr=$1{\times}10^{-3}$, weight decay $1{\times}10^{-2}$, cosine decay), Gumbel-Sigmoid mask generation ($\tau$: $1.0 \to 0.1$), and adaptive Lagrangian sparsity control as the KL and BCE predictors.
240	The only difference is the loss target: per-layer $\|\mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{g}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)}) - \mathbf{y}_{\text{dense}}^{(\ell)}\|_2^2$, replacing the end-to-end KL divergence.
241	This controlled comparison isolates the effect of output-distribution alignment: all three predictors see identical data in identical order with identical architectures; only the loss function differs. % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42
242	
243	MSE uses $\lambda_{\max}{=}1000$ (vs.\ KL's 5000) because MSE's per-layer gradient magnitude is approximately $5\times$ larger than KL's end-to-end gradient, requiring proportionally less penalty pressure to achieve comparable sparsity control. Both reach their target sparsity within 110 steps.
244	At 50\% target, MSE converges to the target sparsity for seed 42; however, with seed 123, sparsity reaches only 5.1\% under the same $\lambda_{\max}$ cap (eval PPL$=$15{,}789, random-level), indicating high sensitivity to initialization---a failure mode not observed with KL-based losses.
245	At 30\%, MSE achieves 27.7\% actual sparsity for s42 (gap 2.3pp).
246	The 3-seed MSE mean (18.52$\pm$1.68; s42=20.43, s123=17.73, s456=17.39) is still $1.7\times$ worse than TEAL (10.82), confirming that the performance deficit stems from the loss function, not insufficient sparsity pressure.
247	
248	
249	\section{Extended SNR Analysis}
250	\label{app:snr_extended}
251	
252	Figure~\ref{fig:snr_trajectory} plots gradient SNR across the full 1000-step training for both KL and BCE predictors, measured at 14 checkpoints (LLaMA-3.1-8B, C4) with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals.
253	From step 100 onward, BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL (2.3--17$\times$ depending on training phase), with the gap narrowing toward convergence ($2.3\times$ at step 1000: $2.026{\times}10^{-3}$ vs.\ $8.78{\times}10^{-4}$). % exp_id: snr_full_curve_1000steps
254	Despite KL's noisier gradient signal, KL-trained predictors produce substantially better masks (Table~\ref{tab:main}), confirming that \emph{target alignment} matters more than gradient clarity for mask quality.
255	BCE's higher SNR reflects its simpler per-neuron classification objective, which concentrates gradients on individual neuron decisions; KL's coupled gradient distributes signal across all mask decisions jointly, yielding lower SNR but discovering compositionally superior mask patterns (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}).
256

260	\caption{Gradient SNR trajectory across 1000 training steps. BCE (orange) maintains consistently higher SNR than KL (blue) from step 100 onward (2.3--17$\times$), yet KL produces superior masks (Table~\ref{tab:main}), suggesting that target alignment matters more than gradient clarity for mask quality.}
261	\label{fig:snr_trajectory}
262	\end{figure}

298	\section{Compensation Training Convergence}
299	\label{app:comp_convergence}
300	
301	All compensation networks~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} were trained for 1000 steps (lr=$10^{-4}$, batch size 4, seq length 2048). Training loss plateaus by step 700 across all valid runs: 30\% s42 (final loss 0.70), 30\% s456 (0.68), 50\% s42 (1.07), with fluctuations remaining within 0.08 loss units for the final 300 steps. The compensation network converges but provides no quality improvement over the KL predictor alone, confirming that the redundancy result reflects predictor self-sufficiency rather than insufficient compensation training. % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42

467	\section{Taxonomy Discussion}
468	\label{app:taxonomy}
469	
470	\paragraph{Correction granularity.}
471	\emph{Static} methods~\cite{xu2025spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.
472	\emph{Per-token} methods~\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.
473	
474	\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}
475	\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.
476	\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.
477	\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass; each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.
478	
479	SPON~\cite{xu2025spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\times$ coupled).
480	DejaVu~\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.
481	D2DMoE~\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.
482	We define \emph{output-distribution alignment} as whether the training loss optimizes a quantity that composes across layers.
483	KL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which does not suffer from error composition; per-layer MSE minimizes reconstruction error independently at each layer, but layer-wise optima do not compose---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\ell{+}1 \ldots L$.
484	The SNR analysis (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}) corroborates this: KL achieves the best masks despite $2.3\times$ lower SNR than BCE, while MSE produces the worst masks.
485	The framework predicts that losses operating on output-level quantities should outperform intermediate-level losses, a hypothesis consistent with our MSE results and testable with alternative output-level objectives.
486	
487	\section{Oracle Mask Provenance}
488	\label{app:oracle_provenance}
489	
490	C1's oracle masks are extracted from a converged KL predictor and binarized via hard threshold (logits $> 0$, equivalent to $\sigma > 0.5$); they reflect one specific training trajectory (optimizer state, data order, Gumbel noise).
491	The s42/s123/s456 consistency ($\leq$1.70pp) provides partial evidence of robustness, but does not rule out trajectory-dependent confounds.
492	
493	\section{SPON Orthogonality}
494	\label{app:spon}
495	
496	SPON~\cite{xu2025spon} bias correction is weakly correlated with the KL predictor (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038) and yields only 2.4\% PPL improvement (12.08$\to$11.79); the predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42
497	The decomposition confirms that SPON and the KL predictor operate on largely orthogonal error sources.
498

553	\section{Per-Layer Sparsity Constraint Analysis}
554	\label{app:constraint}
555	
556	To test the causal role of the KL predictor's learned per-layer allocation, we evaluate two inference-time interventions at 30\% overall sparsity (seed 42, uniform allocation baseline):
557	
558	\begin{table}[h]
559	\centering
560	\caption{Per-layer sparsity constraint analysis. \emph{Protect late}: cap L22--L28 sparsity $\leq$30\%; \emph{Remove valley}: override L14--L18 sparsity to 30\%. Budget redistributed to maintain 30\% overall.}
561	\label{tab:constraint}
562	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
563	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
564	\toprule
565	Condition & PPL & ARC-c & MMLU & GSM8K \\
566	\midrule
567	KL baseline (s42) & 8.82 & 40.8 & 40.6 & 7.4 \\
568	Protect late layers & 9.26 & 42.4 & 40.7 & 6.5 \\
569	Remove valley & 8.76 & 39.3 & 44.0 & 9.0 \\
570	\bottomrule
571	\end{tabular}}
572	\end{table}
573	
574	Protecting late layers (L22--L28) does not improve knowledge-intensive metrics despite reducing their sparsity from 48--57\% to $\leq$30\%, while worsening PPL by 5.2\%.
575	Conversely, removing the protection valley (forcing L14--L18 to 30\% instead of 14--18\%) improves MMLU by +3.4pp and GSM8K by +1.6pp at the cost of ARC-c $-$1.5pp.
576	The KL predictor's learned allocation thus represents a task-dependent trade-off---prioritizing commonsense reasoning via middle-layer protection---rather than a uniformly optimal pattern.
577	This allocation trade-off also explains the $K{=}4$ MMLU anomaly (\S\ref{app:k4_downstream}): $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity (std=0.003) inherently removes the valley, producing MMLU=43.5 comparable to the remove-valley condition (44.0).
578

641	4 & 8.89$\pm$0.01 & 40.0$\pm$1.2 & 66.9$\pm$0.2 & 42.7$\pm$1.4 & 7.0$\pm$1.6 & 67.8$\pm$1.0 & 74.5$\pm$0.5 \\

648	
649	PPL exhibits a non-monotonic U-shape (Figure~\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}): $K{=}2$ is worst (10.75$\pm$0.15), likely due to insufficient per-layer updates (${\sim}$62 updates/layer vs.\ ${\sim}$250 at $K{=}8$). $K{=}16$ achieves the lowest PPL (8.67$\pm$0.02), marginally better than E2E (8.81$\pm$0.02). $K{=}16$ MMLU exhibits high seed variance (std=2.9; s42=44.0, s123=40.0, s456=38.7), with the 3-seed mean (40.9) indistinguishable from E2E (40.7$\pm$1.3).
650	The signal type gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\ per-layer MSE 18.52$\pm$1.68) of 9.47 PPL exceeds the coupling breadth range within KL (best $K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\ worst $K{=}2$ 10.75 = 2.08 PPL), reinforcing that output-distribution alignment, not coupling scope, is the primary quality lever for PPL at this sparsity level. % exp_id: k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed, perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed
```

```json
      "content": "\\appendix\n\n\\section{Inference Efficiency}\n\\label{app:latency}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:latency} reports inference throughput on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB; batch size 1, sequence length 2048, fp16, averaged over 100 forward passes).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Inference latency on LLaMA-3.1-8B (RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell, batch=1, seq=2048, fp16, 100 forward passes). Overhead is relative to dense inference.}\n\\label{tab:latency}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Sparsity & ms/fwd & tok/s & Overhead \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 0\\% & 119.11$\\pm$0.57 & 17{,}195 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nKL predictor-only & 30\\% & 126.64$\\pm$0.70 & 16{,}172 & +6.3\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\nKL predictor-only & 50\\% & 126.78$\\pm$0.31 & 16{,}154 & +6.4\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\n\\midrule\nPredictor + compensation & 30\\% & 129.94$\\pm$0.75 & 15{,}761 & +9.1\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\nPredictor + compensation & 50\\% & 129.71$\\pm$0.53 & 15{,}789 & +8.9\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe full KL-sparse pipeline adds 6.4\\% overhead to dense inference (126.78 vs.\\ 119.11\\,ms/forward), of which the predictor's 32-layer forward pass contributes 3.17\\,ms (2.7\\%); the remainder arises from mask application and sparse FFN indexing.\nEliminating the compensation network saves an additional $\\sim$2.7\\% latency overhead and 67.2M parameters (the per-layer GEMV required for output rescaling); output-distribution KL thus provides both quality and efficiency advantages over BCE-based approaches.\nThe overhead is near-identical at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity because the predictor runs the same computation regardless of target sparsity; only the top-$k$ threshold changes.\n\nNote that the current implementation uses unstructured element-wise masks, which do not translate to wall-clock speedup without sparse kernel support.\nThe pipeline adds $\\sim$6.4\\% overhead; net acceleration requires kernel-level sparse computation optimizations (e.g., Triton block-sparse, cuSPARSE) that are beyond the scope of this work.\nOur contribution is prediction quality and inference simplification (removing the compensation network), not end-to-end latency reduction.\n\n\n\\section{Predictor Bottleneck Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:db_sensitivity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:db_sensitivity} reports the effect of predictor bottleneck dimension $d_b$ on PPL at 50\\% sparsity under both TEAL global allocation (the paper's primary inference protocol) and per-token evaluation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Predictor bottleneck sensitivity at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$). TEAL global is the primary inference protocol; per-token shown for reference.}\n\\label{tab:db_sensitivity}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$d_b$ & Params & TEAL global & $\\Delta$ & Per-token \\\\\n\\midrule\n64 & 38.2M (0.48\\%) & 12.076 & +0.9\\% & 10.16 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n128 & 75.9M (0.95\\%) & 11.963 & --- & 9.218 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n256 & 151.5M (1.89\\%) & 12.105 & +1.2\\% & 8.586 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n512 & 302.5M (3.77\\%) & 12.315 & +2.9\\% & 7.93 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder TEAL global allocation, increasing $d_b$ beyond 128 provides no benefit (PPL slightly worsens), while per-token evaluation shows monotonic improvement (10.16$\\to$7.93).\nThis divergence indicates that the global top-$k$ allocation mechanism, not predictor capacity, is the binding constraint: additional predictor capacity produces better per-neuron scores, but the global ranking and budget distribution cannot exploit them.\nThe $d_b{=}128$ configuration (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base model) is therefore optimal for the TEAL inference protocol.\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Schedule Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:tau_sensitivity}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temperature schedule sensitivity at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, TEAL global).}\n\\label{tab:tau_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau$ Schedule & PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFixed $\\tau{=}0.5$ & \\textbf{11.861} \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\nLinear $1.0 \\to 0.1$ (default) & 11.963 \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\nFixed $\\tau{=}0.1$ & 12.072 \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTemperature schedule has minimal impact ($<$1.8\\% PPL variation).\nThe linear annealing default is a conservative choice; even removing annealing entirely (fixed $\\tau{=}0.5$) yields comparable performance.\n\n\\subsection{Training Convergence}\n\\label{app:training_convergence}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training convergence at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, TEAL global).}\n\\label{tab:training_convergence}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{cc}\n\\toprule\nSteps & PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n200 & 15.158 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n400 & 12.538 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n600 & 12.046 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n800 & 11.907 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n1000 & \\textbf{11.906} \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTraining converges rapidly: 95\\% of the quality gap closes by step 600, and performance saturates by step 800 ($\\Delta < 0.001$ between 800 and 1000 steps).\nOur default of 1000 steps provides a conservative margin.\n\n\n\\section{Geometric Penalty Schedule Dynamics}\n\\label{app:lagrangian}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:lagrangian} reports the convergence of the geometric penalty schedule across sparsity targets.\nThe geometric doubling pattern ($1 \\to 2 \\to 4 \\to 8 \\to \\ldots$) enables $\\lambda$ to reach steady state within $\\sim$10 steps; the deadzone ($\\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\\pm$2\\% of the target.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Geometric penalty schedule convergence across sparsity targets (margin $\\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}\n\\label{tab:lagrangian}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nTarget $\\rho$ & $\\lambda_{\\max}$ & Final $\\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42\n40\\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42\n50\\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42\n70\\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.\nAt 30\\% and 70\\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\\lambda$ to its respective caps.\nThe 40\\% target converges to $\\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\\% deviation; this precision reflects a regime where the target is reachable without $\\lambda$ saturation.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Lagrangian controller dynamics for 30\\% sparsity target ($\\lambda_{\\max}=5000$). Left axis (log scale): penalty weight $\\lambda$ rises from $0.01$ to its cap once actual sparsity undershoots the target at $\\sim$step~150, exhibiting bang-bang oscillations before saturating. Right axis: actual sparsity (solid) converges from the natural density ($\\approx$0.41) toward the target (dashed) and stabilizes near 0.28.}\n\\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTemperature $\\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.\nA mild loss increase in the final $\\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\\tau \\to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, consistent with convergence toward the hard-mask inference regime.\n\nThe controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.\nFor the 30\\% target, actual sparsity is $\\approx$28.5\\% (5\\% deviation). This does not affect conclusions: TEAL at 28.5\\% actual sparsity yields PPL 10.30 (vs.\\ 10.97 at exact 30\\%), a 0.67 PPL difference negligible relative to the KL vs.\\ TEAL gap (8.96 vs.\\ 10.97).\n\n\n\\section{Out-of-Distribution Evaluation}\n\\label{app:ood}\n\nTo address concerns about data homogeneity between training (WikiText-103) and evaluation (WikiText-2), we evaluate on C4~\\cite{raffel2020c4} validation set under TEAL global allocation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Out-of-distribution evaluation on C4 validation (PPL$\\downarrow$). 30\\% KL reports 2-seed mean$\\pm$std; other entries use seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:ood}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Method & C4 PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 9.21 \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & KL predictor (Ours) & \\textbf{16.03$\\pm$0.01} \\\\ % exp_id: c4_ood_kl30_s42, c4_ood_kl30_s123\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%} & KL predictor (Ours) & \\textbf{18.40} \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c4_ood_kl50\n& TEAL vanilla & 38.75 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt the 30\\% sparsity level, the KL predictor achieves C4 PPL 16.03$\\pm$0.01 (2-seed, global allocation, $1.74\\times$ dense), confirming OOD generalization.\nAt 50\\%, the KL predictor achieves 52.5\\% relative advantage over TEAL (18.40 vs.\\ 38.75), comparable to the 50\\% advantage on WikiText-2 (11.92 vs.\\ 23.90), confirming that KL-trained predictions generalize across distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Uniform Allocation.}\nUnder uniform allocation at 30\\%, C4 PPL across three seeds is 18.22 (s42), 16.37 (s123), 16.36 (s456), yielding mean 16.98$\\pm$1.07 ($1.84\\times$ dense). % exp_id: c4_ood_kl30_uniform_3seed\nThe cross-seed variance is substantially higher than in-domain: CV=6.3\\% on C4 vs.\\ 0.2\\% on WikiText-2 (PPL 8.81$\\pm$0.02), driven by seed 42's elevated C4 PPL (18.22 vs.\\ 16.37 for other seeds).\nGlobal allocation's cross-layer budget flexibility partially compensates for domain shift (PPL 16.03 global vs.\\ 16.98 uniform), consistent with the Limitations observation that the predictor partially overfits to training-domain activation patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream Task Robustness.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:ood_downstream} compares downstream performance when the predictor generates masks from C4 text (OOD) vs.\\ WikiText (in-domain), both at 30\\% uniform allocation (seed 42). All six benchmarks show $\\Delta$$\\leq$1.1pp, confirming that predictor masks are robust to calibration domain shift. % exp_id: c4_ood_downstream_kl30, bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_uniform_hellaswag_piqa\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Downstream task robustness to calibration domain shift (KL 30\\% uniform, seed 42). WikiText: predictor masks from in-domain text; C4: masks from OOD text. Metrics: acc\\_norm (ARC-c, HS, PIQA), acc (WG, MMLU), exact\\_match (GSM8K).}\n\\label{tab:ood_downstream}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nCalibration & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\nWikiText & 40.8 & 68.6 & 40.6 & 7.4 & 70.9 & 74.8 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_uniform_hellaswag_piqa\nC4 (OOD) & 40.4 & 69.3 & 41.4 & 6.3 & 70.7 & 74.0 \\\\ % exp_id: c4_ood_downstream_kl30\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$ & $-$0.4 & +0.7 & +0.8 & $-$1.1 & $-$0.2 & $-$0.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Retention Analysis}\n\\label{app:retention}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{per_layer_retention_chart.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer neuron retention under global top-$k$ allocation at 30\\%, 50\\%, and 70\\% sparsity. Dashed lines indicate uniform retention baselines. Late layers (L24--31) are consistently starved due to long-tailed activation distributions, with retention dropping to 28.4\\% (L31) even at 30\\% sparsity. At 70\\%, starvation extends to mid-layers (L10--12: 16.7--20.1\\%), explaining the transition to the capacity-limited regime.}\n\\label{fig:retention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Training Stability}\n\\label{app:stability}\n\nThree-seed evaluation ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$) at 50\\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \\pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\\%); training is stable across random initializations. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\nDownstream metrics are similarly stable: ARC-c $33.8 \\pm 1.2$\\%, WinoGrande $62.9 \\pm 0.1$\\%, MMLU $28.0 \\pm 0.6$\\%, GSM8K $2.2 \\pm 0.1$\\%.\n\nUnder global allocation at 50\\%, MMLU is $28.3 \\pm 0.7$\\%; the near-random performance is capacity-limited rather than seed-sensitive ($\\sigma$=0.7pp vs.\\ 9.5pp at 30\\% global). % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, kl50_global_s123_downstream_v2, kl50_global_s456_eval\nThe low standard deviation across both allocation strategies indicates that the KL training landscape is well-conditioned for this predictor architecture, with low sensitivity to initialization.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Mask Consistency}\n\\label{app:cross_seed_jaccard}\n\nTo assess whether KL predictors discover consistent mask patterns across initializations, we compute pairwise Jaccard distances among three independently trained KL predictors (s42/s123/s456) at 30\\% uniform allocation (100 sequences, WikiText-2 validation). % exp_id: cross_seed_jaccard_kl30\n\nThe mean cross-seed Jaccard distance is 0.363 (IoU = 0.637), substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (0.46--0.68), confirming that KL-discovered patterns form a distinct cluster from magnitude-based masks.\nPer-layer divergence follows a U-shaped pattern: lowest at embedding-adjacent layers (L0: 0.33), peaking at early layers (L1: 0.41), with a mid-network valley (L12--14: 0.33). The random baseline Jaccard distance is 0.462.\n\nWhile specific mask configurations vary moderately across seeds (Jaccard distance 36.3\\%), the resulting PPL is highly stable (8.81$\\pm$0.02, CV=0.2\\%), suggesting multiple near-equivalent optima in the activation mask space.\nThe cross-seed divergence (36.3\\%) is substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (46--68\\%), confirming that KL discovers effective patterns that cluster distinctly from magnitude-based selection.\n\n\n\\section{MSE Baseline Configuration}\n\\label{app:mse_config}\n\nThe MSE predictor uses identical architecture (two-layer MLP, bottleneck $d_b{=}128$), training data (WikiText-103, 1000 steps), optimizer (AdamW, lr=$1{\\times}10^{-3}$, weight decay $1{\\times}10^{-2}$, cosine decay), Gumbel-Sigmoid mask generation ($\\tau$: $1.0 \\to 0.1$), and adaptive Lagrangian sparsity control as the KL and BCE predictors.\nThe only difference is the loss target: per-layer $\\|\\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)}(\\tilde{\\mathbf{m}}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}) - \\mathbf{y}_{\\text{dense}}^{(\\ell)}\\|_2^2$, replacing the end-to-end KL divergence.\nThis controlled comparison isolates the effect of output-distribution alignment: all three predictors see identical data in identical order with identical architectures; only the loss function differs. % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42\n\nMSE uses $\\lambda_{\\max}{=}1000$ (vs.\\ KL's 5000) because MSE's per-layer gradient magnitude is approximately $5\\times$ larger than KL's end-to-end gradient, requiring proportionally less penalty pressure to achieve comparable sparsity control. Both reach their target sparsity within 110 steps.\nAt 50\\% target, MSE converges to the target sparsity for seed 42; however, with seed 123, sparsity reaches only 5.1\\% under the same $\\lambda_{\\max}$ cap (eval PPL$=$15{,}789, random-level), indicating high sensitivity to initialization---a failure mode not observed with KL-based losses.\nAt 30\\%, MSE achieves 27.7\\% actual sparsity for s42 (gap 2.3pp).\nThe 3-seed MSE mean (18.52$\\pm$1.68; s42=20.43, s123=17.73, s456=17.39) is still $1.7\\times$ worse than TEAL (10.82), confirming that the performance deficit stems from the loss function, not insufficient sparsity pressure.\n\n\n\\section{Extended SNR Analysis}\n\\label{app:snr_extended}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:snr_trajectory} plots gradient SNR across the full 1000-step training for both KL and BCE predictors, measured at 14 checkpoints (LLaMA-3.1-8B, C4) with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.\nFrom step 100 onward, BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL (2.3--17$\\times$ depending on training phase), with the gap narrowing toward convergence ($2.3\\times$ at step 1000: $2.026{\\times}10^{-3}$ vs.\\ $8.78{\\times}10^{-4}$). % exp_id: snr_full_curve_1000steps\nDespite KL's noisier gradient signal, KL-trained predictors produce substantially better masks (Table~\\ref{tab:main}), confirming that \\emph{target alignment} matters more than gradient clarity for mask quality.\nBCE's higher SNR reflects its simpler per-neuron classification objective, which concentrates gradients on individual neuron decisions; KL's coupled gradient distributes signal across all mask decisions jointly, yielding lower SNR but discovering compositionally superior mask patterns (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{snr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient SNR trajectory across 1000 training steps. BCE (orange) maintains consistently higher SNR than KL (blue) from step 100 onward (2.3--17$\\times$), yet KL produces superior masks (Table~\\ref{tab:main}), suggesting that target alignment matters more than gradient clarity for mask quality.}\n\\label{fig:snr_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Extended Downstream Evaluation}\n\\label{app:downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:downstream} reports HellaSwag and PIQA across sparsity levels and allocation strategies.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Extended downstream benchmarks (LLaMA-3.1-8B). HellaSwag: 10-shot normalized acc; PIQA: 0-shot normalized acc. KL 30\\% uniform: 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; others: seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Method & HellaSwag & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & \\textbf{82.35} & \\textbf{80.90} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{30\\%}\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{71.07$\\pm$0.54} & \\textbf{74.95$\\pm$0.25} \\\\ % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform (3-seed)\n& TEAL uniform & 63.31 & 72.80 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal30_uniform_6task\n& BCE isocompute & 62.81 & 74.21 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_isocompute_30u_hellaswag_piqa\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{50\\%}\n& KL global & \\textbf{58.95} & \\textbf{69.48} \\\\ % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 56.66 & 69.10 \\\\ % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl50_uniform\n& TEAL uniform & 39.35 & 63.66 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal50_uniform_6task\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 30\\%, the KL predictor retains 86.3\\% of dense HellaSwag accuracy (71.07$\\pm$0.54 vs.\\ 82.35, 3-seed) and 92.6\\% of PIQA (74.95$\\pm$0.25 vs.\\ 80.90), confirming moderate degradation on non-reasoning tasks.\nAt 50\\%, global allocation outperforms uniform on HellaSwag (+2.29pp), consistent with the budget-concentration benefit observed for PPL in the capacity-limited regime (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}); PIQA shows a smaller gap (+0.38pp).\n\n\n\\section{Compensation Training Convergence}\n\\label{app:comp_convergence}\n\nAll compensation networks~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} were trained for 1000 steps (lr=$10^{-4}$, batch size 4, seq length 2048). Training loss plateaus by step 700 across all valid runs: 30\\% s42 (final loss 0.70), 30\\% s456 (0.68), 50\\% s42 (1.07), with fluctuations remaining within 0.08 loss units for the final 300 steps. The compensation network converges but provides no quality improvement over the KL predictor alone, confirming that the redundancy result reflects predictor self-sufficiency rather than insufficient compensation training. % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42\n\n\n\\section{Qwen-2.5-14B Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:14b}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Qwen-2.5-14B at 30\\% nominal sparsity. Actual sparsity differs: KL 29.6\\% (3-seed), TEAL 29.3\\% (target 30\\%). \\textbf{Bold}: best sparse per column per group. each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nD2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.\nWe define \\emph{output-distribution alignment} as whether the training loss optimizes a quantity that composes across layers.\nKL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which does not suffer from error composition; per-layer MSE minimizes reconstruction error independently at each layer, but layer-wise optima do not compose---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\\ell{+}1 \\ldots L$.\nThe SNR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}) corroborates this: KL achieves the best masks despite $2.3\\times$ lower SNR than BCE, while MSE produces the worst masks.\nThe framework predicts that losses operating on output-level quantities should outperform intermediate-level losses, a hypothesis consistent with our MSE results and testable with alternative output-level objectives.\n\n\\section{Oracle Mask Provenance}\n\\label{app:oracle_provenance}\n\nC1's oracle masks are extracted from a converged KL predictor and binarized via hard threshold (logits $> 0$, equivalent to $\\sigma > 0.5$); they reflect one specific training trajectory (optimizer state, data order, Gumbel noise).\nThe s42/s123/s456 consistency ($\\leq$1.70pp) provides partial evidence of robustness, but does not rule out trajectory-dependent confounds.\n\n\\section{SPON Orthogonality}\n\\label{app:spon}\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} bias correction is weakly correlated with the KL predictor (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038) and yields only 2.4\\% PPL improvement (12.08$\\to$11.79); the predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\nThe decomposition confirms that SPON and the KL predictor operate on largely orthogonal error sources.\n\n\\section{Per-Layer KL Downstream Decomposition}\n\\label{app:perlayer_downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:perlayer_downstream} reports raw downstream numbers for the signal type vs.\\ coupling scope decomposition at 30\\% sparsity (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}). BoolQ's $\\approx$100\\% coupling is an upper bound confounded by high cross-seed variance ($\\pm$11.5pp for per-layer KL); the $K{=}4$ pilot (Table~\\ref{tab:k4_downstream}) suggests much of this gap is train--inference mismatch.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{2D decomposition at 30\\% sparsity (LLaMA-3.1-8B, uniform allocation). All rows are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std. Signal type = per-layer KL $-$ MSE; coupling = E2E KL $-$ per-layer KL. Percentages are upper bounds (dimensions not fully orthogonal; see \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).}\n\\label{tab:perlayer_downstream}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & BoolQ \\\\\n\\midrule\nE2E KL & 8.81$\\pm$0.02 & 43.7 & 68.9 & 40.7 & 7.5 & 73.7 \\\\\nPer-layer KL & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 39.9 & 65.9 & 38.6 & 5.8 & 64.5 \\\\\nMSE & 18.52$\\pm$1.68 & 35.8$\\pm$4.1 & 59.8$\\pm$2.6 & 30.7$\\pm$2.6 & 2.3$\\pm$0.7 & 64.5$\\pm$5.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTotal gap & 9.71 & 7.9 & 9.1 & 10.0 & 5.2 & 9.2 \\\\\nSignal type (\\%) & 97.5 & 51.9 & 67.0 & 79.0 & 67.3 & $\\approx$0 \\\\\nCoupling (\\%) & 2.5 & 48.1 & 33.0 & 21.0 & 32.7 & $\\approx$100 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{$K{=}4$ Mismatch Control}\n\\label{app:k4_downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:k4_downstream} reports 3-seed $K{=}4$ per-layer KL results at 30\\% sparsity.\nTraining with $K{=}4$ random layers per step reduces the train--inference mismatch (4/32 vs.\\ 1/32 layers masked during training, while inference applies all 32).\nThe gap closure column reports the fraction of the $K{=}1 \\to$ E2E KL gap closed by $K{=}4$.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{3-seed $K{=}4$ mismatch control at 30\\% sparsity (LLaMA-3.1-8B, uniform allocation, $s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$). Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform). Gap closure $= (K{=}4 - K{=}1) / (\\text{E2E} - K{=}1) \\times 100$.}\n\\label{tab:k4_downstream}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& $K{=}4$ (3-seed) & $K{=}1$ (3-seed) & E2E KL (3-seed) & Gap closure (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPPL$\\downarrow$ & 8.89$\\pm$0.01 & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 8.81$\\pm$0.02 & 66.7 \\\\\nARC-c & 40.0$\\pm$1.3 & 39.9 & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 2.6 \\\\\nWG & 66.9$\\pm$0.2 & 65.9 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & 33.3 \\\\\nMMLU$^\\ast$ & 42.7$\\pm$1.4 & 38.6 & 40.7$\\pm$1.3 & $>$100 \\\\\nGSM8K & 7.0$\\pm$1.6 & 5.8 & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 & 70.6 \\\\\nBoolQ$^\\dagger$ & 67.7$\\pm$7.5 & 64.5$\\pm$11.5 & 73.7 & 34.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^\\ast$MMLU $K{=}4$ systematically exceeds E2E across all 3 seeds; attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform per-layer sparsity (std=0.003) matching uniform eval allocation. Independently confirmed: removing the predictor's middle-layer valley yields MMLU=44.0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}).\n$^\\dagger$BoolQ exhibits high inter-seed variance for both $K{=}4$ (std=7.5) and $K{=}1$ (std=11.5); gap closure unreliable.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Sparsity Constraint Analysis}\n\\label{app:constraint}\n\nTo test the causal role of the KL predictor's learned per-layer allocation, we evaluate two inference-time interventions at 30\\% overall sparsity (seed 42, uniform allocation baseline):\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-layer sparsity constraint analysis. \\emph{Protect late}: cap L22--L28 sparsity $\\leq$30\\%; \\emph{Remove valley}: override L14--L18 sparsity to 30\\%. Budget redistributed to maintain 30\\% overall.}\n\\label{tab:constraint}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCondition & PPL & ARC-c & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nKL baseline (s42) & 8.82 & 40.8 & 40.6 & 7.4 \\\\\nProtect late layers & 9.26 & 42.4 & 40.7 & 6.5 \\\\\nRemove valley & 8.76 & 39.3 & 44.0 & 9.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nProtecting late layers (L22--L28) does not improve knowledge-intensive metrics despite reducing their sparsity from 48--57\\% to $\\leq$30\\%, while worsening PPL by 5.2\\%.\nConversely, removing the protection valley (forcing L14--L18 to 30\\% instead of 14--18\\%) improves MMLU by +3.4pp and GSM8K by +1.6pp at the cost of ARC-c $-$1.5pp.\nThe KL predictor's learned allocation thus represents a task-dependent trade-off---prioritizing commonsense reasoning via middle-layer protection---rather than a uniformly optimal pattern.\nThis allocation trade-off also explains the $K{=}4$ MMLU anomaly (\\S\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity (std=0.003) inherently removes the valley, producing MMLU=43.5 comparable to the remove-valley condition (44.0).\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Sparsity Distribution}\n\\label{app:perlayer_sparsity}\n\nThe KL predictor at 30\\% target sparsity learns a non-uniform per-layer allocation that differs markedly from magnitude-based selection.\nAggregate sparsity is similar across broad regions (early L0--L7: KL 28.1\\% vs.\\ magnitude 27.5\\%; late L24--L31: 44.9\\% vs.\\ 47.2\\%), but middle layers (L8--L23) exhibit high internal variance despite similar averages (28.2\\% vs.\\ 27.3\\%).\nTable~\\ref{tab:perlayer_sparsity} highlights three sub-ranges.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-layer sparsity sub-ranges: KL predictor vs.\\ magnitude (LLaMA-3.1-8B, 30\\%, uniform allocation). Ranges denote min--max across layers in each group.}\n\\label{tab:perlayer_sparsity}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nLayer Range & KL (\\%) & Mag (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nL8--L12 (high pruning) & 32--36 & 25--26 & $+$7 to $+$10 \\\\\nL14--L18 (protection valley) & 14--18 & 28--30 & $-$8 to $-$16 \\\\\nL22--L28 (aggressive pruning) & 48--57 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{C4 Training Data Control}\n\\label{app:c4_control}\n\nTo test whether WikiText-103's narrow domain drives the knowledge-task regression (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), we train the KL predictor on C4~\\cite{raffel2020c4} (diverse web text) under identical hyperparameters (1000 steps, 30\\% uniform, s42).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{C4-trained vs.\\ WikiText-trained KL predictor (LLaMA-3.1-8B, 30\\% uniform, s42).}\n\\label{tab:c4_control}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nTraining Data & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS / PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\nWikiText-103 & 8.82 & 40.78 & 68.59 & 40.6 & 7.35 & 70.85 / 74.81 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_uniform_hellaswag_piqa\nC4 & 11.55 & 43.09 & 68.67 & 39.95 & 4.09 & 69.24 / 75.03 \\\\ % exp_id: c4_kl30_s42_eval\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe C4-trained predictor achieves comparable MMLU (40.0\\% vs.\\ 40.6\\%) but worse GSM8K (4.1\\% vs.\\ 7.4\\%) and higher PPL (11.55 vs.\\ 8.82), despite training on more diverse text.\nThis rules out training data composition as the primary cause of knowledge-task regression and supports the hypothesis that forward KL's mode-covering property structurally under-protects rare reasoning-critical activations. % exp_id: c4_control_eval\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Coupling Breadth Analysis}\n\\label{app:k_curve}\n\nWe vary the number of layers coupled per training step ($K$) from 1 (pure per-layer) to 32 (end-to-end), evaluating all predictors under TEAL uniform allocation at 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{K-curve: coupling breadth vs.\\ predictor quality (30\\%, TEAL uniform, LLaMA-3.1-8B). All values are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (s42/s123/s456) except $K{=}1$ downstream (single seed). ``---'' = not evaluated.}\n\\label{tab:k_curve}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{cccccccc}\n\\toprule\n$K$ & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 39.9 & 65.9 & 38.6 & 5.8 & --- & --- \\\\\n2 & 10.75$\\pm$0.15 & 39.7$\\pm$1.2 & 63.2$\\pm$1.7 & 38.3$\\pm$3.2 & 2.8$\\pm$0.6 & 66.5$\\pm$1.0 & 73.5$\\pm$0.7 \\\\\n4 & 8.89$\\pm$0.01 & 40.0$\\pm$1.2 & 66.9$\\pm$0.2 & 42.7$\\pm$1.4 & 7.0$\\pm$1.6 & 67.8$\\pm$1.0 & 74.5$\\pm$0.5 \\\\\n8 & 9.00$\\pm$0.06 & 40.7$\\pm$1.0 & 68.7$\\pm$1.3 & 40.4$\\pm$0.6 & 6.8$\\pm$1.3 & 69.0$\\pm$1.1 & 75.1$\\pm$1.1 \\\\\n16 & 8.67$\\pm$0.02 & 41.9$\\pm$1.1 & 68.5$\\pm$0.9 & 40.9$\\pm$2.9 & 8.5$\\pm$1.2 & 70.1$\\pm$0.4 & 75.0$\\pm$0.8 \\\\\n32 (E2E) & 8.81$\\pm$0.02 & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & 40.7$\\pm$1.3 & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nPPL exhibits a non-monotonic U-shape (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}): $K{=}2$ is worst (10.75$\\pm$0.15), likely due to insufficient per-layer updates (${\\sim}$62 updates/layer vs.\\ ${\\sim}$250 at $K{=}8$). $K{=}16$ achieves the lowest PPL (8.67$\\pm$0.02), marginally better than E2E (8.81$\\pm$0.02). $K{=}16$ MMLU exhibits high seed variance (std=2.9; s42=44.0, s123=40.0, s456=38.7), with the 3-seed mean (40.9) indistinguishable from E2E (40.7$\\pm$1.3).\nThe signal type gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\\ per-layer MSE 18.52$\\pm$1.68) of 9.47 PPL exceeds the coupling breadth range within KL (best $K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\\ worst $K{=}2$ 10.75 = 2.08 PPL), reinforcing that output-distribution alignment, not coupling scope, is the primary quality lever for PPL at this sparsity level. % exp_id: k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed, perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed\n\n\\section{Licenses}\n\\label{app:licenses}\n\nLLaMA-3.1-8B is released under the Llama 3.1 Community License Agreement.\nMistral-7B-v0.3 is released under the Apache 2.0 License.\nQwen-2.5-14B is released under the Apache 2.0 License.\nWikiText-103 and WikiText-2~\\cite{merity2017pointer} are released under the Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike License (CC BY-SA 3.0).\nAll evaluation benchmarks (ARC, WinoGrande, MMLU, GSM8K, HellaSwag, PIQA) are publicly available under their respective licenses.\nOur code will be released under the MIT License upon acceptance.\n\n\\section{Use of AI Assistants}\n\\label{app:ai-assistants}\n\nWe used Claude (Anthropic) as an AI coding and writing assistant\nthroughout this work. Specifically:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Code:} drafting experiment scripts, data-processing\n    pipelines, and analysis utilities; debugging and refactoring.\n    \\item \\textbf{Writing:} drafting and revising paper sections,\n    polishing prose, and reformatting LaTeX.\n    \\item \\textbf{Literature:} summarizing related papers to inform\n    related-work section.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
      "numLines": 675,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 675
    }
  },
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BdW3z49MfTDp7e5ugV7agA`</summary>

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:5:CATS~\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} and GRASP~\cite{shin2025grasp} predict activation sparsity patterns without additional training.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:9:OWL~\cite{yin2024owl} determines per-layer sparsity ratios from outlier distributions for weight pruning.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:20:PowerInfer~\cite{song2024powerinfer} and PowerInfer-2~\cite{xue2024powerinfer2} exploit power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU/NPU scheduling.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:21:MaskLLM~\cite{fang2024maskllm} shares the recipe-level principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:23:Our contribution beyond the shared recipe is analytical: a systematic decomposition of signal type vs.\ coupling scope for sparsity prediction training, via target-swap ablation and per-layer KL controls (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:32:\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:36:MoC~\cite{wu2025moc} selectively activates Top-$K$ FFN channels using SwiGLU's native gating during pretraining.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:39:\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:40:Wanda~\cite{sun2024wanda}, SparseGPT~\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt}, and SliceGPT~\cite{ashkboos2024slicegpt} produce static weight masks via one-shot or structured pruning; Polar Sparsity~\cite{shrestha2025polar} scales contextual sparsity to batched inference by shifting focus from MLP to attention head sparsity; Alizadeh et al.~\cite{alizadeh2024llmflash} leverage activation sparsity for memory-efficient inference via selective flash loading.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:41:Switch Transformers~\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:42:KL-based distillation~\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd,ko2024distillm} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:10:\paragraph{Training.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:12:The KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:85:\paragraph{Target-Swap Ablation: Target Quality vs.\ Gradient Coupling.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:157:To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:166:\caption{Coupling breadth $K$ vs.\ perplexity (30\%, TEAL uniform, 3-seed mean$\pm$std). $K{=}32$ denotes end-to-end training. $K{=}2$ degradation reflects insufficient per-layer updates (${\sim}62$ vs.\ ${\sim}250$ at $K{=}8$).}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:56:The Gumbel noise provides stochastic exploration of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\tau \to 0$, $\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:58:We anneal $\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:64:In our setting, STE produces discontinuous gradient signals: small parameter changes can flip many neurons simultaneously. The resulting oscillations in the penalty weight prevent convergence to the target sparsity.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:83:No temperature scaling ($T{=}1$) is applied~\cite{hinton2015distilling}; we distinguish $T$ from the Gumbel temperature $\tau$ (\S\ref{sec:method:gumbel}), which controls mask sharpness rather than logit scaling.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:137:KL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which composes across layers; per-layer MSE does not---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\ell{+}1 \ldots L$, explaining its $1.7\times$/$9.6\times$ worse PPL than TEAL at 30\%/50\% (50\%: s42 only; \S\ref{sec:experiments}). % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/appendix.tex:244:At 50\% target, MSE converges to the target sparsity for seed 42; however, with seed 123, sparsity reaches only 5.1\% under the same $\lambda_{\max}$ cap (eval PPL$=$15{,}789, random-level), indicating high sensitivity to initialization---a failure mode not observed with KL-based losses.
```
