```text
Now let me do the comprehensive audit. Let me analyze all the dimensions systematically.

## 1. 叙事一致性 (CRITICAL)

### Abstract 的问题
Abstract says: "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use BCE, training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-k ranking."
→ Problem: mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation

### Intro 的问题
Intro says: "How the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs"


→ This is broader than just the BCE/global mismatch—it's about how training objectives fundamentally affect prediction quality. The specific mismatch between per-neuron training and global allocation hasn't been formally studied before.

### Intro Contributions:
The paper makes three main empirical and diagnostic contributions: first, it ranks five different loss functions by how well their output distributions align with actual neuron importance, breaking down the results by layer to show that the gap between signal types is larger than variations in coupling breadth. Second, it provides cross-scale validation across model sizes from 7 to 14 billion parameters, with separate analysis for different task types. Third, it uses target-swap ablations and per-layer controls to suggest that the coupling between training objectives and task performance is task-dependent.

### Method:
The approach uses a KL-based training framework with Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling, a predictor architecture, a geometric penalty schedule, and includes gradient analysis comparing BCE versus KL training, plus a taxonomy of the different coupling patterns.

### Experiments:
The paper validates the main PPL improvements, splits results by task type to show commonsense versus knowledge reasoning behave differently, extends the analysis across longer training runs, examines mask patterns through ablation, decomposes coupling effects per layer, shows that KL-derived masks eliminate the need for compensation mechanisms, and tests the approach across different architectures like Mistral and Qwen.

### Conclusion:
Output-distribution alignment emerges as the primary design lever—KL training reduces perplexity by 14.5% over the baseline at 30% sparsity, with effects varying by task type and signal type gaps dominating over coupling breadth variations.

Now I'm checking that the paper's narrative holds together: the abstract and introduction both identify the core problem of per-neuron BCE allocation versus global optimization mismatch, the four main contributions map cleanly onto the method and experiments (ablation tables, per-layer decomposition, cross-architecture validation, and compensation redundancy analysis), and the method's use of KL directly addresses the gap identified in the introduction.

The experiments support all the key claims—output-distribution alignment emerges as the primary design lever, KL delivers the stated PPL gains, task-type dependence is validated, and the K-curve analysis backs up the signal-type gap finding.

But I'm noticing some inconsistencies in the reported numbers: the abstract cites 14.5% PPL reduction at 30% matched sparsity with specific values, while the introduction gives the same percentage but at 28.5% sparsity with slightly different numbers.  Looking at the main table, TEAL's uniform performance is 10.82, not the 10.30 mentioned in the intro—and the table caption clarifies that at the nominal 30% target, actual sparsity differs between methods (KL hits 28.5%, TEAL hits 29.3%), so I need to figure out which baseline number corresponds to which sparsity level.

The 10.30 figure appears to be TEAL evaluated at 28.5% actual sparsity to match KL's sparsity for a fair comparison, which explains the 14.5% improvement claim in both the abstract and intro—they're comparing the KL predictor against TEAL at the same actual sparsity level, not at the nominal 30% target.

Now I'm checking the other claims in the abstract, particularly the MMLU regression figure. The −5.2pp difference is comparing KL's performance (40.7±1.3) against TEAL's (45.86), not against the dense baseline, so that checks out.

For Qwen-14B's GSM8K performance, the −19.9pp gap between KL uniform (32.2±5.3) and TEAL uniform (52.1) is verified.

The compensation redundancy claim in section 4.5 also holds up. Now I'm looking at the mask divergence claim of 46-68% from magnitude-based targets—the abstract cites this range, and I can see the Jaccard distance averaging 68% at 50% sparsity in section 4.3, with cross-seed variation showing values in the 0.46-0.68 range.

For the target-swap ablation, I need to verify that BCE trained on KL-derived targets achieves near-KL performance with a PPL gap of ≤0.65pp at 30% sparsity, and I'm checking the C1 table to confirm the actual numbers. I'm noticing a discrepancy between the abstract's claim of "≤0.65pp" and what the actual data shows—the maximum PPL gap across the three seeds is 0.63pp, not 0.65pp, so there's a slight inconsistency in how the results are being reported. the s42 gap from the table is 0.63pp, not 0.65pp. The footnote mentions "s42 oracle from earlier checkpoint (gap 0.65pp)", which might be from a different evaluation run. The table shows 9.45 - 8.82 = 0.63, so there's a minor rounding discrepancy here, but the abstract's ≤0.65pp claim is still technically correct.

Now checking the per-layer controls claim about signal type gap versus coupling breadth range. The math checks out: 18.52 - 9.05 = 9.47 PPL for the signal type gap, and 10.75 - 8.67 = 2.08 PPL for the coupling breadth range, confirming the abstract's statement that the former exceeds the latter.

The wall-clock speedup caveat about needing sparse kernel support aligns with what's stated in the introduction. And I'm verifying the cross-architecture evaluation claims on Mistral and Qwen models...

Looking at how "consistently" is used throughout the paper. The SNR claim in method.tex is backed by actual figure data showing 2.3-17x differences, so that's a concrete measurable fact. The MMLU and GSM8K underperformance claim has data from both 8B and 14B scales, which makes it acceptable even if just two points. The appendix uses are similar and all have supporting data.

For "leverage" in related work, it's a single straightforward usage describing what prior work does.

Now checking the em-dashes: most of the `---` occurrences are table placeholders which are fine, but there are a few explanatory em-dashes scattered through method.tex and appendix.tex that don't align with formal paper conventions.

The limitations section at 118 words covers four key points and fits what EMNLP expects.

For the abstract, I need to compress it down to just a few sentences of clear prose. The abstract checks out on word count and covers all the key elements—problem statement, motivation, proposed solution, and results—though it's quite dense with technical details and numbers that make it harder to parse. Moving to the introduction, it follows the expected structure with background on SwiGLU and learned sparsity predictors, identifies the core mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference budgets, and distinguishes the approach from prior work like MaskLLM. Looking at the contributions now, I'm checking whether each claim maps cleanly to a section or table. The four main contributions track well—the output-distribution ranking ablation ties to Table 5 and the per-layer analysis, the cross-scale evidence spans the architecture and task-type splits, the target-swap diagnostic is in the ablations, and the KL-mask finding removes the need for compensation. But there are two methods sections that don't have explicit contribution callouts: the geometric penalty schedule and gradient analysis are described in the body but never framed as standalone contributions.

The title and abstract alignment looks solid—everything centers on SwiGLU models across LLaMA, Mistral, and Qwen, and the contextual sparsity prediction claim matches the per-token masking work. Now checking the strength of the quantitative claims: the 14.5% reduction is grounded in the actual numbers, and the statement about KL underperforming holds across both the 8B and 14B scales tested.

The paper is careful with its language throughout—using "directional" for downstream effects, acknowledging the train-inference mismatch as a confound, and saying "suggests" rather than making definitive causal claims. No overreaching "state-of-the-art" language either.

On the experimental side, error bars are present for multi-seed runs with single-seed entries clearly marked, and TEAL's deterministic nature is noted. The baselines include recent work like TEAL and adapted versions of DejaVu, so the comparison points are reasonable.

The ablations are thorough—testing different divergence measures, gradient estimators, training strategies, and key hyperparameters like temperature and bottleneck dimension. However, some recent methods discussed in related work like R-Sparse, CATS, and LaRoSA aren't experimentally compared. For reproducibility, the paper provides learning rates, batch sizes, sequence lengths, training steps, optimizer details, and weight decay settings, plus GPU specifications.

Now looking at the notation—the symbols are used consistently throughout: binary masks, soft masks from Gumbel relaxation, importance scores, sparsity ratios, and temperature parameters all maintain their definitions. Wall-clock training time isn't explicitly stated though, which would be useful for reproducibility.

For terminology, there's a minor inconsistency with how the output-distribution objective is phrased across sections, and "coupling breadth" versus "coupling scope" appear somewhat interchangeably, though the intent remains clear.

The paper flows naturally from introduction through related work into the methodology and experiments. Rather than having a dedicated discussion section, the paper embeds discussion points within the experiments section through analysis of task-type splits and allocation behavior, which works fine for this format.

Looking at the limitations section now—it covers about 118 words with four substantive points: the sparsity sweet spot being limited to 30%, knowledge-intensive regression issues, evaluation scope gaps like missing MT-Bench and long context tests, and baseline coverage gaps including the 30% BCE isocompute baseline and WikiText train/eval overlap. These are visible AI writing markers according to the paper-writing norms and should be removed. I'm noticing a potential narrative drift between the introduction's framing and what the experiments actually show. The intro positions this as solving a mismatch problem that should improve everything, but the results reveal a more complex picture—uniform allocation works best for downstream tasks while the KL predictor mainly helps with perplexity, and there are tradeoffs like commonsense gains paired with knowledge regression. The paper does acknowledge this nuance, but the intro's framing could mislead readers about the scope of the improvements. The real issue is that the intro frames the mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference as the central problem, but the experiments reveal that signal type (output distribution vs intermediate) actually matters far more than coupling—the signal type gap is 9.47 PPL versus only 2.08 PPL for coupling breadth. So there's a narrative misalignment where the intro emphasizes what turns out to be the less important factor, even though the contributions list does acknowledge the signal type finding.

Paragraph 4 of the intro is trying to do too much at once—it layers in cross-architecture results, a scoping statement about being empirical, wall-clock limitations, and a defensive novelty claim all in a few lines, making it hard to parse.

The abstract's 14.5% perplexity reduction claim needs scrutiny around what denominator is being used. The footnote adequately explains the sparsity discrepancy and directs readers to the appendix, so the disclosure is clear. The experiments section frames the comparison well by establishing that uniform allocation preserves performance while global allocation reveals allocation-quality tradeoffs. I'm noticing that several results lack error bars—TEAL uniform is deterministic so that's fine, but BCE global and the 50% entries are single-seed runs without uncertainty estimates.

Now looking more closely at the second contribution about cross-scale evidence and the knowledge regression claim. The C4 control experiment is clever—showing that switching from WikiText to C4 doesn't fix the regression suggests the problem stems from the optimization objective rather than the training data itself. But the abstract frames this conclusion more definitively than the evidence really supports; it could equally be that both datasets have similar issues, or something else entirely is driving the regression. The paper does hedge this somewhat in the main text.

Turning to the abstract's density—it's packed with seven sentences full of numbers and technical details that might be hard for some reviewers to parse. The four required questions are covered, but the information density could be overwhelming.

Let me examine the "consistently" claims more carefully. "BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL throughout training" holds up well since it's measured across 1000 training steps. For "KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales," the evidence covers 8B and 14B models plus Mistral, though the Mistral MMLU result is tied rather than a clear loss.

Now I'm checking the Introduction's central claim about output-distribution alignment outperforming per-neuron classification. The statement uses "consistently and substantially outperforms," but this depends on the metric—PPL shows consistent improvement across three models, yet downstream tasks are mixed, with commonsense gains but knowledge task losses. A reviewer might push back on whether PPL should be the primary quality measure when downstream accuracy matters more.

The conclusion does acknowledge this nuance, noting that gains are task-type dependent and recommending KL for commonsense/PPL while TEAL works better for knowledge tasks. I'm also verifying the contribution claims: the ablation covers five losses (Forward KL, JSD, Reverse KL, MSE, BCE), which matches what's stated, and the STE variants are relaxation methods rather than separate losses.

Now I'm noticing the intro's phrasing "output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses" is a bit ambiguous—it could mean measuring how well each loss achieves output-distribution alignment, but MSE and BCE aren't really output-distribution losses themselves. The wording would be clearer as "comparison across five losses" since the five losses span different categories (decomposed, intermediate, and coupled approaches) rather than all being output-distribution variants.

Let me verify the cross-references in the abstract. The §sec:perlayer label is correctly defined in experiments.tex, and the notation n=3 for random seeds is standard, so those check out. Now I'll write up the final audit.

Looking at the regression numbers in the abstract—the "directional at n=3" qualifier makes sense because with only 3 seeds, those results aren't statistically significant yet. I should verify the Related Work section follows a consistent structure with its thematic organization and paragraph headings.

The Training-Free Activation Sparsity section does list out what each paper contributes—TEAL, CATS, R-Sparse, DIP, SparseInfer, GRASP, LaRoSA, and others—but it redeems itself with a synthesizing sentence that contrasts their fixed-statistics approach against this work's input-dependent predictor. The Weight Pruning and Routing paragraph has a similar listing feel, though the Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity section reads more naturally. The listing pattern is pretty standard in ML papers given how many related works exist, but the synthesis at the end keeps it from being purely "A did X, B did Y."

Now I'm finalizing the audit and organizing by severity. There's nothing critical that would trigger a desk reject—the narrative flows well overall. Though I should flag the em-dashes throughout the introduction and methods as a potential concern since they're a known AI writing marker, even if they won't cause outright rejection. Let me also verify that claim about the PPL gap being ≤0.65pp against what Table 3 actually shows.

Looking at the specific results, the maximum gap appears to be 0.63pp from one sample, which does satisfy the stated bound of 0.65pp. But there's a footnote mentioning a different oracle evaluation that yields 0.65pp, so there's a minor discrepancy between what the table reports and what the abstract claims. I'll flag this as a minor inconsistency worth noting. I'm noticing a small discrepancy between the footnote's reported gap of 0.65pp for s42 and what the table shows (0.63pp), likely due to rounding or different evaluation runs. The abstract's claim of "≤0.65pp" is technically correct since 0.63 ≤ 0.65. I'm also verifying the typography—the en-dashes in "train--inference mismatch" and "46--68%" are correctly used for connecting concepts and numeric ranges in LaTeX. Now I'm ready to compile the structured output. beyond what the experiments strictly prove, since the paper only tested a few tasks. But it's a reasonable inference from the consistent pattern and is hedged with "suggesting." ✓

The paragraph structure looks solid throughout—introduction, methods, and experiments all maintain reasonable lengths without unnecessary fragmentation. Now I'm checking a specific claim in the introduction about TEAL's perplexity reduction at matched sparsity, but the sentence seems to cut off mid-thought with the downstream results notation.

Looking at the full comparison, the PPL improvement over TEAL is strong at 14.5%, but the downstream MMLU score of 40.7% is far below dense performance at 65.3%—a 24.6 percentage point gap. I think the authors are making an honesty point here: the perplexity gains don't translate fully to downstream accuracy, showing that 30% sparsity significantly degrades knowledge tasks. Comparing PPL against TEAL but downstream against dense is a bit inconsistent though, and it could come across as misleading to mix those baselines in the same sentence.

Now I'm checking where the 7.3× cross-seed variance amplification for global top-k comes from.  The uniform MMLU standard deviation is 1.3, while global MMLU is 9.5, so dividing gives exactly 7.3. That checks out.

The abstract's claim about BCE trained on KL-derived targets is also accurate—it specifies the 3-seed, 30% condition where this holds, and the C1 table confirms this, though the 50% case is only tested on a single seed so it's appropriately not claimed there.

For the per-layer controls claim, I'm verifying that the signal type gap of 9.47 PPL is indeed larger than the coupling breadth range of 2.08 PPL at 30%, though there's a confounding factor from the train-inference mismatch that complicates the interpretation.

Now I'm noticing something about how the regression results are presented—the abstract gives very specific numbers like −5.2pp and −19.9pp but then qualifies them as "directional at n=3", which creates a tension between the precision of the figures and the preliminary nature of the findings. Presenting such exact numbers alongside a directional qualifier could mislead readers into thinking these are more robust than they actually are.

There's also a clarity issue with mixing different model sizes in the same parenthetical—8B MMLU results next to Qwen-14B GSM8K results makes it harder to parse at a glance, though the model labels are technically there.

Let me do a final pass on the Limitations section to make sure it's complete and well-structured. I'm noticing some formatting inconsistencies in the manuscript—there are em-dashes used in prose that should be checked. Looking through the intro and methods sections, I see several instances where em-dashes appear in explanatory text, like the key finding about output-distribution alignment outperforming per-neuron classification, and another about how KL-discovered masks diverge substantially from magnitude targets. There's also one in the methods section about MSE not being sufficient, and a few more scattered through the appendix. Let me make sure these are all consistent before finalizing.

I'm also double-checking the usage of "consistently" throughout the paper—some instances describe measurable facts across training steps, which seems fine, while others describe observed performance patterns across different benchmarks that I should verify more carefully.

The trickiest case is in the introduction where "consistently and substantially outperforms" claims to hold across all conditions, but the downstream results are actually mixed—commonsense reasoning shows the pattern while knowledge tasks don't. That one in the main text is the most problematic since reviewers will scrutinize it closely.

Now checking the "five losses" claim against the ablation table. There are actually eleven different configurations listed, not five—the paper seems to be grouping them by the core loss function type rather than counting each variant separately.
```
